What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I came in because I can’t stop feeling like everything that goes wrong is my fault. Like at work, if something gets delayed, even if it’s not really on me, I still feel like I’m a bad employee. And then at home too—like with the house, there’s always something, you know, repairs or bills, and I start thinking I’m a bad homeowner and maybe I don’t deserve to keep any of it.\n\nSorry, I know that’s kind of a lot. It’s just been sitting on me all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if “seen things” exactly, but I’ve heard a voice sometimes. It’s usually like a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, or once I thought it was through the air vent, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.

It’s not constant… I wouldn’t say I hear it all day or anything. It happens sometimes, usually when I’m at home, and it’ll sound like a woman in the hallway or through the air vent saying something like, “You’re going to lose it all.”

But, yeah… not every day, exactly, just often enough that I don’t really feel like I can ignore it.

Like, I’ll be standing there in my socks by the door, and then I’m thinking about how the neighbors probably think I’m strange because I’m always trying to look normal when I run into them by the mailboxes… sorry, that’s not really the point. But yes, I’ve heard that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the voice?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant… I wouldn’t say I hear it all day or anything. It happens sometimes, usually when I’m at home, and it’ll sound like a woman in the hallway or through the air vent saying something like, “You’re going to lose it all.”\n\nAnd then I get stuck on it because it sounds so real, like I’ll actually go check outside to see if someone was there. Which is embarrassing, because then I start thinking about who saw me doing that, and the neighbors already kind of look at me funny sometimes—sorry. But, yeah… not every day, exactly, just often enough that I don’t really feel like I can ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voice even when you're feeling a little better and able to function more normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. There was a stretch of a few weeks where my mood wasn’t as heavy, like I was getting through work more normally and answering things and not falling behind as much.\n\nBut even then I still heard the voice near the vents. And I was still pretty sure the mortgage company was watching me somehow, and that my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. I know that sounds… I don’t know. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. No, not really. I mean, my mood got a little lighter for a few weeks—like I was getting through work more normally, keeping up with emails and tasks better—but the voice didn’t exactly stop.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… I don’t think so. There was that stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t as weighed down and I was getting through work more normally—like keeping up with things better, not just staring at my inbox forever—but I still heard the voice near the vents.

And I still had that feeling that the mortgage company was watching me, and at work I kept thinking my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. So… even when my mood was lighter, I don’t think there was a full two weeks where it all stopped.

I mean… I used to be the person who planned brunches with friends, and I’d paint in the spare room, and I liked hiking when the weather was decent. Positive? No… not really. It’s usually more like a woman’s voice, like from the hallway or the air vent, saying things like, “You’re going to lose it all.”

Then it’s just… back to that heavy feeling again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. And I kept telling myself after the next thing was done I’d feel normal again, but… the sadness and not really caring about things stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to be the person who planned brunches with friends, and I’d paint in the spare room, and I liked hiking when the weather was decent. Like I’m pretending to care because I remember that I used to care. Even if I go, I’m mostly just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself a lot, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of all over the place. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not only then. I mean, I wish it worked that way, like it was just when I was really low and then it would lift with everything else, but… there was a stretch of several weeks where my mood wasn’t as heavy. I was getting through work more normally, answering things, keeping up better.

So… no, it wasn’t just when I was sad or down.

It’s just been sitting on me all the time.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, when I look back on it, it’s not like it comes and goes in a normal way. The sadness has been there almost the whole time since this started, and I don’t really get interested in things the way I used to.\n\nThere are little stretches where I can kind of function better, like get through work or keep up with stuff around the house, but it doesn’t last very long. Then it’s just… back to that heavy feeling again.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a little over two years, I think. About two years and one month.\n\nI remember trying to pin it down because there was a work project around then, and I kept thinking I was just tired from that, but then it didn’t lift. And I kept telling myself after the next thing was done I’d feel normal again, but… the sadness and not really caring about things stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to be the person who planned brunches with friends, and I’d paint in the spare room, and I liked hiking when the weather was decent. Like I’m pretending to care because I remember that I used to care. Even if I go, I’m mostly just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself a lot, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of all over the place. Most days I don’t really eat proper meals during the day, and then I’ll realize I’ve basically had coffee, maybe toast, and then whatever’s easiest later. Like, I’ll stand in the kitchen and just not want to deal with making anything. It’s weird because I used to meal prep sometimes—well, not perfectly, but I had containers and everything—and now I just sort of… forget, or avoid it, and then suddenly my jeans don’t fit the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt completely better from these feelings of sadness and lack of interest, only to have them return later?\n\nPatient: Um… not really completely better, no. Sometimes it’s quieter and I can function better for a bit, but I don’t think it ever fully went away and then came back fresh.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had two whole months where I felt totally like myself again. I mean, there were little stretches where I could sort of keep up better—like I’d answer emails on time or make plans and actually show up—but the low mood and the not really enjoying things was still kind of there underneath.\n\nIt’s like… some weeks I could pass better as normal, if that makes sense, but looking back it never fully lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if “seen things” exactly, but I’ve heard a voice sometimes. And I kept telling myself after the next thing was done I’d feel normal again, but… the sadness and not really caring about things stayed.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy before you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I used to be the person who planned brunches with friends, and I’d paint in the spare room, and I liked hiking when the weather was decent. I even used to sign up for networking events, which sounds boring, but I actually kind of enjoyed meeting people.\n\nNow all of that feels like… chores, almost. Like I’m pretending to care because I remember that I used to care. Even if I go, I’m mostly just getting through it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight since you started feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t weigh myself a lot, so I can’t give you an exact number, but my clothes have definitely been looser.\n\nMy appetite’s been kind of all over the place. Most days I don’t really eat proper meals during the day, and then I’ll realize I’ve basically had coffee, maybe toast, and then whatever’s easiest later. Like, I’ll stand in the kitchen and just not want to deal with making anything. It’s weird because I used to meal prep sometimes—well, not perfectly, but I had containers and everything—and now I just sort of… forget, or avoid it, and then suddenly my jeans don’t fit the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you had periods in your life where you felt completely better from these feelings of sadness and lack of interest, only to have them return later?\n\nPatient: Um… not really completely better, no. Like, there have been stretches where I could kind of keep up more—go to work, answer texts, do the normal things without it feeling quite as impossible.\n\nBut looking back, the sadness and the not-caring feeling have pretty much been there almost the whole time since it started. It’s more like the volume changes, if that makes sense. Sometimes it’s quieter and I can function better for a bit, but I don’t think it ever fully went away and then came back fresh.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of at least two months where you felt completely back to your normal self?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think I’ve had two whole months where I felt totally like myself again. I mean, there were little stretches where I could sort of keep up better—like I’d answer emails on time or make plans and actually show up—but the low mood and the not really enjoying things was still kind of there underneath.\n\nIt’s like… some weeks I could pass better as normal, if that makes sense, but looking back it never fully lifted for that long.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever heard voices or seen things that others couldn't hear or see?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if “seen things” exactly, but I’ve heard a voice sometimes. It’s usually like a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, or once I thought it was through the air vent, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all.” And it feels so real in the moment that I’ve actually gone outside to check who was there. Like, I’ll be standing there in my socks by the door, and then I’m thinking about how the neighbors probably think I’m strange because I’m always trying to look normal when I run into them by the mailboxes… sorry, that’s not really the point. But yes, I’ve heard that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you hear the voice?\n\nPatient: It’s not constant… I wouldn’t say I hear it all day or anything. It happens sometimes, usually when I’m at home, and it’ll sound like a woman in the hallway or through the air vent saying something like, “You’re going to lose it all.”\n\nAnd then I get stuck on it because it sounds so real, like I’ll actually go check outside to see if someone was there. Which is embarrassing, because then I start thinking about who saw me doing that, and the neighbors already kind of look at me funny sometimes—sorry. But, yeah… not every day, exactly, just often enough that I don’t really feel like I can ignore it.\n\nDoctor: Do you hear the voice even when you're feeling a little better and able to function more normally?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. There was a stretch of a few weeks where my mood wasn’t as heavy, like I was getting through work more normally and answering things and not falling behind as much.\n\nBut even then I still heard the voice near the vents. And I was still pretty sure the mortgage company was watching me somehow, and that my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. I know that sounds… I don’t know. I was functioning better, but that part didn’t really go away.\n\nDoctor: Did the voice and the feelings of being watched go away when you were feeling a little better?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, my mood got a little lighter for a few weeks—like I was getting through work more normally, keeping up with emails and tasks better—but the voice didn’t exactly stop.\n\nI still heard it near the vents, and I still felt pretty sure the mortgage company was watching me somehow. And at work, I kept thinking my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. Even when I was functioning better, that part was still there.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you didn't hear the voice for at least two weeks when your mood was lighter?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. There was that stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t as weighed down and I was getting through work more normally—like keeping up with things better, not just staring at my inbox forever—but I still heard the voice near the vents.\n\nAnd I still had that feeling that the mortgage company was watching me, and at work I kept thinking my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
They happen at other times too. There was, um… a stretch of a few weeks where I wasn’t as down, like I could get through work more normally and keep up with things.

But even then I still heard the voice near the vents. And I was still convinced the mortgage company was watching me somehow, and that people at work were monitoring what I was doing. It’s hard to explain because vents already make noises, you know, so I’d start thinking about that, but… no, it wasn’t only when I was sad.

It’s usually like a woman’s voice, and it sounds like it’s coming from the hallway, or once I thought it was through the air vent, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nShe’ll say things like, “You’re going to lose it all.” And it feels so real in the moment that I’ve actually gone outside to check who was there. I mean, my mood got a little lighter for a few weeks—like I was getting through work more normally, keeping up with emails and tasks better—but the voice didn’t exactly stop.\n\nI still heard it near the vents, and I still felt pretty sure the mortgage company was watching me somehow. And at work, I kept thinking my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. Even when I was functioning better, that part was still there.\n\nDoctor: Did you ever have a period where you didn't hear the voice for at least two weeks when your mood was lighter?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. There was that stretch of several weeks where I wasn’t as weighed down and I was getting through work more normally—like keeping up with things better, not just staring at my inbox forever—but I still heard the voice near the vents.\n\nAnd I still had that feeling that the mortgage company was watching me, and at work I kept thinking my coworkers were monitoring what I was doing. So… even when my mood was lighter, I don’t think there was a full two weeks where it all stopped.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you heard the voice and it told you something positive?\n\nPatient: Positive?
